The norm controls the two estimates actually
used in this proposition. On a fixed
finite-dimensional perturbation family all
higher smooth seminorms are finite and are
handled separately in compact-family
regularity statements.

\subsection{Paths with only birth--death singularities}
Enlarge $F$ to include the monomials of degree at
most five in the trace coordinates $F_0$.
These remain cylinder functions: a monomial
is a smooth invariant function of the joint
finite holonomy family.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:generic-path}
Two regular primary data in $\mathfrak B$
can be joined within a common $\mathcal U(F)$
by a smooth path with finitely many singular
parameters. At each singular parameter
there is one quadratic tangency, with
versal normal form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:birth-death}
 S_t(x,y)=x^3+\sigma(t-t_*)x+Q(y),
 \qquad\sigma\in\{1,-1\},
\end{equation}
where $Q$ is nondegenerate. All other
intersections are transverse.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Start with the segment from $h_a$ to $h_b$
and perturb it to
\[
 h_t=(1-t)h_a+th_b+\beta(t)c,
\]
where $\beta$ vanishes near the endpoints
and is positive on the interior where
singularities may occur. The segment is
compact in the open convex ball, so small
$c$ keeps the path in the ball.

Variation of the holonomy function induces
an exact Lagrangian deformation; its
Hamiltonian on the perturbed Lagrangian is
the restriction of that function to the
connection moduli space, by the Lagrangian
cobordism of \cite[Proposition~2.18]{DFL}. The trace
coordinates remain embedding coordinates
by Proposition~\ref{prop:small-ball}.
Polynomials of degree at most three in
embedding coordinates realize arbitrary
third jets at one point. Polynomials of
degree at most five realize arbitrary
second jets at two distinct points. For
the latter assertion, choose an affine function $\ell$ with
$\ell(a)=0$ and $\ell(b)=1$. Given a second jet at $b$, multiply
$\ell^3$ by the quadratic Taylor polynomial at $b$ of that jet
divided by $\ell^3$. The product has degree at most five, realizes
the prescribed jet at $b$, and vanishes to order three at $a$.
Use $(1-\ell)^3$ in the same way for the jet at $a$, then add
the two polynomials. Restriction of ambient jets
to an embedded submanifold is surjective.

In a Weinstein chart, varying one
Lagrangian changes the relative generating
function by this Hamiltonian to first
order. The map on its jets is triangular
with identity diagonal. Parametric
transversality therefore excludes higher
singularities, two simultaneous tangencies,
and tangencies with zero crossing speed:
these strata have codimension at least two.
A generic one-parameter path meets only
the codimension-one birth--death stratum,
transversely. Its parameter-dependent
splitting normal form is
\eqref{eq:birth-death}.

The singular-parameter set is closed by
compactness of the Lagrangians. Each
singular parameter is isolated by the
normal form, and the endpoint neighborhoods
are regular. It is consequently finite.
No uniform separation of the two points
in the two-point jet argument is required;
transversality is applied on the open
configuration space of distinct pairs.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:persistent}
Suppose $H(C_G(M))\ne0$. The path in
Lemma~\ref{lem:generic-path} has a finite
subdivision with regular subdivision
points such that each subinterval carries
a continuous transverse intersection
point throughout the subinterval.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Every regular primary datum has at least
one intersection. Otherwise its gauge
complex would have no generators by
Proposition~\ref{prop:small-ball}(iv),
contradicting invariance and nonvanishing
of the gauge homology.

At a birth--death time the local pair
in~\eqref{eq:birth-death} is absent on
one side. If it were the only intersection
at the singular time, compactness would
force the nearby regular intersection set
on that side to be empty. Thus another
intersection exists at that time and is
transverse. The implicit function theorem
continues it across the wall.

On a closed interval with no walls, the
incidence set of intersections is a
compact one-manifold projecting locally
diffeomorphically to the interval. It is
a finite covering, hence a disjoint union
of sections. Extend the chosen transverse
point from each wall to neighboring
regular subdivision times. This gives
the required finite collection of
persistent sections.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Constancy of the sphere character]
\label{thm:character-constancy}
Fix a splitting, metric data and gauge
base, and suppose $H(C_G(M))\ne0$. Then
$\chi^P(S_h)$ is independent of the
regular datum $P$ with primary data in
$\mathfrak B$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
First remove the regularizing data at
both endpoints using
Lemma~\ref{lem:pointed-character}. Join
the primary data by the subdivided path
of Lemma~\ref{lem:persistent}. On each
subinterval the pointed character is
constant along its persistent transverse
point. At a regular subdivision time the
pointed character equals the global
character for any choice of point, so
one may switch to the persistent point
for the next subinterval. Chaining these
equalities gives constancy along the
whole path. Restore the endpoint
regularizing data. The same argument
allows variation of the compatible
symplectic almost-complex structure at
a fixed gauge base.
\end{proof}

The argument uses nonvanishing only to
find a point persisting through each
birth--death. It does not assume that
individual generators persist along an
arbitrary path, or that the regular
locus of the perturbation ball is
connected.

\section{Reflection of the transported orientations}
\label{sec:reflection}

\subsection{Reflection and sphere gluing}
The reflected presentation $\bar p$
interchanges the two compression bodies.
A strip is reflected by
\[
 \bar u(s,t)=u(-s,1-t).
\]
This map preserves the orientation of the
strip domain and keeps the surface
almost-complex structure. It reverses
the translation direction. The target
symplectic manifold retains the original
orientation; the reversed order of the
Lagrangians supplies the dual complex.
Mixed solutions themselves are not
identified by this formula. The
orientation at $\bar p$ is transported
through its own mixed domain.

For a strip from $x$ to $y$, the
reflection of its coherent orientation
has differential sign
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reflection-sign}
 \operatorname{sign}_{\rm dual}(\bar u)
   =-(-1)^{|y|}\operatorname{sign}(u).
\end{equation}
The minus sign is the reversed
translation vector; the other sign is
the graded transpose. In general the
graded transpose of a strip of index $n$
carries the sign $(-1)^{n|w|}$, where $w$ is
the target of the strip read on the
presentation whose system is being
transposed. Thus this reflected
system is the one for which the
paired generator bases give the graded
dual differential.

Attaching $S_h$ changes the index by four.
The determinant factor at a sphere node
is complex and even. The additional
transpose sign is $(-1)^{4|y|}=1$.
Consequently reflection of a coherent
strip system preserves its sphere
character. What remains is to compare
this reflected system with the system
transported through the reflected mixed
domain.

\subsection{Capping, unfolding, and end orientations}
We record the end-gluing comparison before using it to compare
sphere characters. For a capped folded configuration $c$ with
outputs $x$ on $\bar p$ and $y$ on $r$, write $F_c$ for its
determinant line after contracting the two normalized auxiliary
cap factors. The conversion line at $Z$ is retained in its fixed
position. Transport from the mixed configuration gives
$\Gamma_c:F_c\to L_{\bar p}(x)\otimes_R L_r(y)$.
Thus this is a tensor with two outgoing ends. Bending the first
end gives a map from $L_{\bar p}(x)^\vee$, not from
$L_{\bar p}(x)$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:fold-coherence}
Let $u$ be any smooth outgoing strip from $x$ to $x'$ on $\bar p$,
and $w$ any smooth outgoing strip from $y$ to $y'$ on $r$.
Capping commutes with their determinant gluing. For $k=0$,
unfolding along the diagonal commutes with the same gluing,
with the first outgoing strip reflected into an incoming strip
by graded transpose. These
statements hold for arbitrary strip indices and configuration
classes.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put $d_u=\det D_u$ and $d_w=\det D_w$. The assertion about the
end systems is the commutative diagram
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
 F_c\otimes d_u\otimes d_w
 &\xrightarrow{\quad(\Gamma_u\otimes_R\Gamma_w)\Gamma_c\quad}&
 L_{\bar p}(x')\otimes_R L_r(y')\\
 \big\downarrow G&&\big\Vert\\
 F_{c\#(u,w)}&\xrightarrow{\qquad\Gamma_{c\#(u,w)}\qquad}&
 L_{\bar p}(x')\otimes_R L_r(y').
\end{array}
\]
In the upper arrow the strip determinant factors are inserted
next to their corresponding generator factors using the right
tensor convention. In particular moving $d_u$ past $L_r(y)$
contributes $(-1)^{\ind(u)|y|}$.

Here $G$ is ordered determinant gluing, including the cap
contractions. Before contraction take the order
\[
 \mathscr I,\quad\det\Theta,\quad
 \det D_c,\quad\det D_{a_0},\quad\det D_{a_1},\quad d_u,\quad d_w,
\]
where $\mathscr I$ is the complete generator input line in cyclic
triangle order and $a_0,a_1$ are the two caps. If a sphere is
attached, append its complex node factor last. Both routes use
this order. Associativity regroups factors without interchanging
them; any permutation needed for an end convention is performed
on both routes with its graded sign. In particular the conversion
line is never carried through the moving configuration determinant.
The unit cap maps contract their paired generator and determinant
factors by even evaluation maps. No assumption that the individual
cap generator has even parity is needed.

First consider the completed mixed triangle at positive scale.
Its localized shift retains the ordinary two-input cobordism and
ends at that cobordism followed by a constant mixed pair. There
ordinary determinant associativity gives the end-gluing diagram,
with the conversion line and the fixed unit cap maps in the order
above. The relative family construction transports it over the
positive-scale configuration families.

Pull back the entire ordered diagram through
Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family}. Its ordinary-gluing compatibility
applies to the strip and cap operators at their ends in the stated
cofinal neck-and-scale range. Only after this diagram commutes do
we cancel the completing vertices and contract the two cap factors.
The fixed-width closed-cut line comparison retains the conversion
factor; its completion is expressed through the separate output
lines through the reference comparison $K_{0,x}$ of
Proposition~\ref{prop:reference-comparison}, not by completing the
fixed closed-$Z$ input itself. This proves the first diagram for
the limiting configurations. It does not shift a limiting triangle
with empty triple intersection to a constant triangle.

For unfolding with $k=0$, first choose doubling data retaining the
Hodge almost-complex structure and zero inhomogeneous germ in the
collapse neighbourhood, the compatible ordinary ends and the fixed
cap models. Choose a folded
configuration obtained from a smooth configuration on the unfolded
strip. The two coefficient systems are then restrictions of one
smooth system. At the $W^{1,p}\to L^p$ realization, $p>2$, equal
traces along the diagonal are exactly the condition for the two
sections to join to a $W^{1,p}$ section after reflecting the lower
side. Restriction is the inverse, and the same identification holds
for $L^p$ target sections. This is an operator identification.
At higher Sobolev orders equal traces alone would not suffice.
Instead identify the higher-order determinant lines through their
common smooth kernels and cokernels, using smooth stabilization
columns. Elliptic regularity makes these identifications commute
with gluing and parameter variation.

The comparison is first made on this doubling locus. Any finite
set of smooth configuration classes has representatives there:
smooth the joined maps in a small collar of the diagonal and keep
the end data fixed. The smoothing is a relative homotopy and
changes no sphere class. Interpolate the auxiliary almost-complex
and perturbation data to doubling data within the admitted data
space, preserving these germs and end values;
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(a) gives the continuous
Fredholm families. Determinant transport extends the resulting
comparison back to the original finite configuration families.
The first gluing diagram and path independence in
Proposition~\ref{prop:transport} make it independent of these
homotopies. With stabilizations off the joining regions the
operator identification on the doubling locus gives
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
 F_c\otimes d_u\otimes d_w&\xrightarrow{G}&F_{c\#(u,w)}\\
 \big\downarrow U_{\rm ord}&&\big\downarrow U\\
 d_{Ru}\otimes\det D_{Uc}\otimes d_w
 &\xrightarrow{G}&\det D_{Ru\#Uc\#w}.
\end{array}
\]
Here $Ru$ runs from $x'$ to $x$ on $p$, so it attaches to the
incoming end of $Uc$, which runs from $x$ to $y$. The left arrow
$U_{\rm ord}$ applies unfolding and reflection and then interchanges
$F_c$ and $d_u$, with sign $(-1)^{|F_c|\ind(u)}$. At the
operator level the maps are restriction, reflection and the same
ordinary-end cutoff patching, whose supports are disjoint from
the diagonal. Their two composites agree. This is a diagram on
the zero-scale section problem; no unfolding map for the
positive-scale gauge operator is asserted, and no additional
commutation of unfolding with collapse is used. The
displayed graded interchange expresses that agreement in the
chosen tensor order. Cancelling the ordered
stabilizations proves the displayed statement. The diagonal
normal factor has its complex orientation and is even.
Reflection of the strip $u$ of index $n$ moves its index line
across the paired endpoint line and contributes the graded transpose
sign. As in~\eqref{eq:reflection-sign}, the graded transpose of a strip
of index $n$ carries the sign $(-1)^{n|w|}$, where $w$ is the target of
the strip read on $p$; at index one this is the sign
in~\eqref{eq:reflection-sign}, and at index four it is the sign
$(-1)^{4|y|}=1$ above. Here $Ru$ runs from $x'$ to $x$ on $p$, so the
sign is $(-1)^{n|x|}$.
For a strip counted modulo translation the reversed translation
vector adds the minus sign in \eqref{eq:reflection-sign}.
To check the total sign, put $a=|x|$, $b=|y|$ and
$f=|F_c|=a+b$. The outgoing gluing sign is $(-1)^{nb}$.
On bending, the reflected input sign $(-1)^{na}$ and the swap
$(-1)^{nf}$ have product $(-1)^{n(a+f)}=(-1)^{nb}$.
Thus the diagrams give the same sign at every index.
\end{proof}

\subsection{One folded comparison}
Fix one admissible fold pair with lower
primary datum $\widetilde p$ and upper
height datum $r$, both in the admissible
ball. Choose the height after the radius
of that ball, as in
Section~\ref{sec:choices}. Double the
folded triangle and attach its auxiliary
caps. Before bending, the resulting disk has two outgoing
strip ends modeled on $\bar{\widetilde p}$ and $r$.
By Lemma~\ref{lem:fold-coherence}, its transported orientation
is coherent with the independently transported strip system on
$\bar{\widetilde p}$ and the transported outgoing system on $r$.
The comparison with the dual system on $p$ is made only after
the sphere characters have been identified.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:fold-character}
\[
 \chi^{\bar{\widetilde p}}(S_h)=\chi^r(S_h).
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Choose a smooth reference configuration
$c_0$ on the doubled disk. Insert a representative sphere on
the first outgoing strip end at a fixed small positive plumbing
parameter. Move this smooth inserted configuration through the
unfolded disk to the second outgoing end, then refold. The two
resulting configurations lie in the same relative class. Under diagonal
unfolding the two sphere
classes are identified with the same generator of the relative
sphere group. In the product description this moves the class
between the two factors modulo the diagonal class. A reversal of
its representative replaces the generator by its negative and
does not change a character with values in $\{1,-1\}$.

There is a continuous orientation on
the determinant bundle along this path.
Its construction on the folded domain
uses the collapse transport of
Section~\ref{sec:collapse}, the
closed-cut conversion, and the
reference comparison of
Proposition~\ref{prop:reference-comparison}. For the endpoint comparisons place
the sphere in an ordinary strip factor attached away from the
collapse neighbourhood. At the fixed positive plumbing parameter,
Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family} commutes with gluing that entire
strip. Its interior-node determinant comparison is the usual
punctured Cauchy--Riemann bordering and complex evaluation map;
the gauge rows are absent on this pure strip. Ordinary-gluing
associativity thus gives the endpoint comparison with the complex
even node factor appended last. The collapse scale and ordinary
neck can be chosen after this plumbing parameter. No simultaneous
node-and-collapse degeneration estimate is used. At the two ends,
the first diagram of Lemma~\ref{lem:fold-coherence}
identifies the transported orientation with both specified
strip-node gluing orientations.
No Koszul sign is introduced by moving
the complex node factor.

It remains to carry the node-glued orientation along the path, on
which the node crosses the image of the diagonal. Carry out the move at
zero collapse scale, on the doubling locus, with the doubling data of
Lemma~\ref{lem:fold-coherence}: the Hodge almost-complex structure and
zero inhomogeneous germ in the collapse neighbourhood. There the folded
operator is identified with the unfolded pure real Cauchy--Riemann
operator, by the $W^{1,p}\to L^p$ identification in the proof of that
lemma, and in the unfolded picture the image of the diagonal is an
interior line, not a matching or boundary collar. Along the whole path
the node lies in a disc separated from the matching and boundary
collars, so Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(c) makes the node gluing
a continuous isomorphism of line bundles along the path. Hence transport
carries the node-glued orientation at the start to the node-glued
orientation at the end. The transported orientation is continuous along
the same zero-scale path by Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family}: the path is
a compact smooth family of limiting configurations, and the collapse
datum is unperturbed near the diagonal. The ratios at the two ends are
therefore equal, giving the assertion.

This comparison uses arbitrary smooth
configurations, not only holomorphic
solutions. To justify coherence where
the fold is attached to prescribed
strip configurations, select one
reference configuration for each of the
finitely many generator pairs and their
required sphere classes. Choose the
height and neck lengths so that
ordinary gluing applies to this finite
set. The determinant-line construction
then extends over each configuration
component by path independence.
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations} and
Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting} are
needed on all the domains in this
finite construction; a comparison only
at the constant solution would not
suffice.
\end{proof}

Changing the almost-complex base on the
closed cut changes the output generator
lines by a fixed diagonal conversion.
That conversion cancels in a sphere
ratio. Thus the character comparison is
unchanged by the conversion required in
Section~\ref{sec:excision}, although that
conversion remains essential for the
cup identity as a chain-level formula.

\begin{corollary}[Reflection invariance]\label{cor:reflection-character}
For every regular datum $p$ in the
admissible ball at an object with
nonzero integral gauge homology,
\[
 \chi^{\bar p}(S_h)=\chi^p(S_h).
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:character-constancy}
on $-M$ to compare $\bar p$ with
$\bar{\widetilde p}$. Apply
Proposition~\ref{prop:fold-character}
to compare the latter with $r$.
Apply character constancy on $M$ to
compare $r$ with $p$. The reflected
ball is the same ball under the
chosen reflection identification.
Gauge duality makes nonvanishing
for $M$ and $-M$ equivalent.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The reflection pairing}
Compare on the strips of $\bar p$ its
own transported system and the dual
system of~\eqref{eq:reflection-sign}.
Their ratio is constant on smooth
configuration components and
multiplicative under concatenation.
Choose one reference intersection
point. A configuration from it to
any other intersection determines
a sign $\kappa_p(x)$. Changing that
configuration changes its class by
a sphere; Corollary
\ref{cor:reflection-character} says
that the ratio on this sphere is
one. Hence the ratio on a strip
from $x$ to $y$ is
$\kappa_p(x)\kappa_p(y)$, independent
of the strip class.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:reflection-pairing}
The map
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Phi-S}
 \Phi_p:\CF(\bar p)\longrightarrow\CF(p)^\vee,
 \qquad o_x^{\bar p}\longmapsto
                \kappa_p(x)(o_x^p)^\vee
\end{equation}
is an even chain isomorphism. The
configuration-level comparison
determines it up to one sign. The
pairings may be chosen so that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Phi-symmetry}
 \Phi_{\bar p}=\Phi_p^\vee\ev.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Equation~\eqref{eq:reflection-sign}
and the ratio just computed give
exactly the dual differential under
\eqref{eq:Phi-S}. The paired gauge
bases make the sum of the two
endpoint parities zero, so the map
is even. Any two comparisons have
constant ratio: configurations
exist between every pair of
intersection points, and the ratio
on all those configurations fixes
the relative generator signs.
Only the initial reference sign
is free.

Reflect twice on a strip of index
$n$ with endpoints $x,y$. The two
transpose signs multiply to
$(-1)^{n(|x|+|y|)}=(-1)^n$.
The corresponding change of the
paired basis is the sign in the
graded evaluation map. Thus the
pairing obtained by the second
reflection is exactly
$\Phi_p^\vee\ev$, after the common
reference sign is fixed. This
proves~\eqref{eq:Phi-symmetry} at
configuration level.
\end{proof}

A chain-map condition alone would
not determine $\kappa_p$ up to
one sign. If the differential
splits into two subcomplexes,
one can change the sign on one
summand and still obtain a chain
map. The preceding construction
uses all strip configurations,
including those which are not
solutions, to exclude that extra
freedom.

Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hat-Phi}
 \widehat\Phi_p=\Phi_p^\vee\ev:
   \CF(p)\longrightarrow\CF(\bar p)^\vee.
\end{equation}
This is the reflection pairing
which will be composed with the
cup map~\eqref{eq:check-cup}.

\section{Continuation and the diagonal compression cup}
\label{sec:continuation}

\subsection{Coherent orientations with prescribed ends}
A continuation datum from $p$ to $q$ has a strip-like
surface with Lagrangian boundary conditions varying
on a compact interval and equal to those of $p,q$
at its ends. The almost-complex and Hamiltonian
data are likewise constant outside that interval.
Throughout this section the primary paths lie in the common convex
ball of Proposition~\ref{prop:small-ball}; after Hamiltonian
identification, their exact-graph primitives lie in a fixed convex
neighbourhood. Homotopies have fixed endpoints and remain in this
neighbourhood. Almost-complex and Hamiltonian continuation data are
varied with these fixed boundary identifications. All statements of
independence below concern this class of data.
For generators $x,y$, let $\mathcal B_{p,q}(x,y)$
be its smooth configuration space, with the
prescribed exponential limits. A coherent
orientation system assigns relative orientations
to the determinant lines over these spaces and
commutes with attaching strips at either end.

The space of configurations in a fixed relative
homotopy class is path connected. One may first
choose a continuous relative homotopy, smooth it
relative to the fixed ends and labels, and then
make it constant beyond common end cutoffs. Local
charts are given by an exponential map for a
metric making the boundary labels totally
geodesic. The topology in
Section~\ref{sec:geometry} makes these paths
continuous. The relative classes are a torsor
for the same sphere group as fixed-label strips,
and the index changes by four under its generator.
In particular there is at most one index-zero
component for fixed endpoints.

\begin{lemma}[Rigidity of coherent systems]\label{lem:rigidity}
Two coherent continuation orientation systems
with the same end systems differ by one sign.
Such a system exists if and only if the sphere
characters at the ends agree.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The ratio of two systems is constant on each
configuration component. If a strip is attached
at either end, the common end orientation cancels,
so the ratio does not change. Choose one
reference configuration from $x_0$ to $y_0$.
For any $x,y$, choose strips from $x$ to $x_0$
and from $y_0$ to $y$. Attaching these strips
transports the ratio to a configuration with
ends $x,y$. Any difference in relative class
can be supplied by a sphere class on either
strip. Since the end systems are the same,
this also leaves the ratio unchanged. Thus
there is only one sign for all configurations.

For existence choose a reference orientation
and extend it by the same gluing procedure.
The determinant bundle on each configuration
component is orientable: the boundary
Lagrangians are the parallelizable compression-
body Lagrangians, and the remaining sphere
factors have complex orientation. The only
compatibility left when the gluing paths are
changed is moving a sphere class from the
incoming end to the outgoing end. The two
extensions agree precisely when the two sphere
characters agree. This also proves necessity
by moving the node of one sphere through a
continuation configuration. The construction
is continuous because the determinant line
is a line bundle over the smooth
configuration space.
\end{proof}

In an index-zero comparison a finite form of
the lemma suffices. Choose one strip for each
input generator and one for each output
generator, in the classes needed to reach
the index-zero component. Coherence on that
finite collection fixes the ratios of all
zero-dimensional counts. This form permits
the height and neck thresholds to be chosen
after a finite set of configurations, without
a claim of uniform estimates over the whole
configuration space.

Theorem~\ref{thm:character-constancy} supplies
existence for $p,q$ in the same admissible
ball. For a lower datum and an auxiliary upper
height datum, the folded configuration itself
supplies a coherent system and hence the
required character equality; moving the node through
the unfolded configuration crosses the image of the
diagonal, and this is justified as in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fold-character}. The latter
observation applies even when the auxiliary
upper datum is outside the character-constancy
ball, provided it satisfies the separate
primary regularity threshold.

\subsection{Parity and continuation maps}
For a continuation configuration $c$ from $x$
to $y$, the number
\[
 d(p,q)=|y|-|x|-\ind D_c\pmod2
\]
is independent of the configuration and its
end generators. It is additive under
composition of continuation data. The
configuration description by sphere classes, together with a
homotopy in the specified small family, shows its independence of
the chosen datum in that family.
For the paired gauge bases of this paper,
$d(p,q)=0$ at the data used in the comparison.
Here one can use the integral cup formula to
check the parity without assuming that
integral nonvanishing implies mod-two
nonvanishing.

Indeed, at a fold pair $(p,r)$ the map
$B(p,r)$ is homogeneous and
\eqref{eq:cup-zero} identifies it up to
homotopy with the even isomorphism
$N_r\cont_G(p,r)n_p$. On nonzero integral
homology this map cannot be both even and
odd. Thus its degree is zero. The doubled
configuration has the same index as the
continuation problem, and the gauge
reflection pairing is even; hence
$d(p,r)=0$. A common upper height datum
and additivity give $d(p,q)=0$. All uses
of the integral cup formula in this
argument precede the diagonal-cup
identification and therefore do not use
the continuation properties being proved.

With a coherent system define
\[
 \cont_S(p,q):\CF(p)\longrightarrow\CF(q)
\]
by the signed zero-dimensional continuation
count. It is even and commutes with the
differential. The compactification of a
one-dimensional continuation moduli space
proves this exactly as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:N-chain}.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:continuation}
The continuation maps satisfy, up to sign
and chain homotopy,
\begin{align}
 \cont_S(q,r)\cont_S(p,q)&\simeq
                 \pm\cont_S(p,r),\label{eq:cont-composition}\\
 \cont_S(p,p)&\simeq\pm1.\label{eq:cont-unit}
\end{align}
Their homotopy classes are independent of
the continuation datum. They are homotopy
equivalences.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For a long strip neck, ordinary gluing
identifies the zero-dimensional solutions
with pairs of continuation solutions.
The determinant gluing is coherent, so
the signed matrix is the composite matrix.
The glued orientation system has the same
end systems as the chosen direct
continuation system. Lemma~\ref{lem:rigidity}
compares them by one sign. Varying the
neck length gives the chain homotopy in
\eqref{eq:cont-composition}.

A homotopy of continuation data, constant
near the ends, gives the corresponding
homotopy by its oriented one-dimensional
boundary. The pure symplectic compactness
and gluing used here are
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(c). For
constant data the zero-dimensional
solutions are constant strips, with
canonical positive orientation. This
normalizes the unit. Apply composition
to a datum and its reverse to obtain a
homotopy inverse.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Reflection of continuation}
There is no translation quotient for a
continuation configuration. Reflecting it
therefore has the graded transpose sign
but no additional translation sign.
Using the pairings of
Proposition~\ref{prop:reflection-pairing}
gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cont-reflection}
 \cont_S(\bar p,\bar q)\simeq
 \pm\Phi_q^{-1}\cont_S(q,p)^\vee\Phi_p.
\end{equation}
To verify coherence of the reflected
system, glue an input strip, a
continuation configuration, and an
output strip, and reflect the three
pieces in reverse order. If their
indices are $n_u,n_c,n_w$, the reordering
sign is
$(-1)^{n_un_c+n_un_w+n_cn_w}$.
Combining it with the three transpose
signs leaves $(-1)^{n_ud(p,q)}=1$.
The generator corrections $\kappa_p$
and $\kappa_q$ identify the reflected
end systems with the own transported
systems. Rigidity then gives the one
sign in~\eqref{eq:cont-reflection}.
This argument also shows why parity
zero is needed here.

\subsection{Identification of the diagonal cup}
For $k=0$ the compression correspondence
is the diagonal. Double the folded
triangle, glue the two distinguished
caps, and unfold along that diagonal.
The resulting surface is a continuation
strip. This is the geometric
identification used in Paper~I. To
obtain an integral statement we compare
its coherent systems before counting.

\begin{proposition}[Diagonal cup]\label{prop:diagonal-cup}
At every admissible fold pair $(p,r)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:diagonal-cup}
 B(p,r)\widehat\Phi_p\simeq
                  \pm\cont_S(p,r).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Use both diagrams of Lemma~\ref{lem:fold-coherence}. After
unfolding, the incoming system is the graded dual of the own
transported system on $\bar p$. Its comparison with the system on
$p$ is exactly the dual configuration-level ratio defining
$\kappa_p$. Indeed for every strip from $x$ to $y$ that ratio is
$\kappa_p(x)\kappa_p(y)$; these are precisely the two endpoint
corrections in $\widehat\Phi_p$. On a homogeneous generator its
coefficient is $(-1)^{|x|}\kappa_p(x)$, since the graded evaluation
map enters \eqref{eq:hat-Phi}. Bending by $B_{\CF(\bar p)}$ supplies
the second factor $(-1)^{|x|}$; the two evaluation signs cancel.
Thus precomposition by
$\widehat\Phi_p$ changes the unfolded input system into the
prescribed system on $p$. The outgoing system is already the
prescribed one on $r$. This is a statement for every configuration
class and every strip index, not only for differential trajectories.

The pulled-back capped system and the chosen continuation system
therefore have the same two end systems. Their relative sign is
constant under the two gluing operations in the diagrams. Rigidity
(Lemma~\ref{lem:rigidity}) applies and leaves one scalar sign. A
nonconstant generator-sign automorphism would change one of those
configuration-level gluing maps; a grading operator would change
an odd-index gluing map. Both have been fixed before invoking
rigidity. This proves the required comparison of coherent systems.

For sufficiently long cap necks,
the gluing theorem gives a bijection
of zero-dimensional solutions and
preserves these orientations. A cap
of index zero has one regular
solution with unit coefficient, so
no extra multiplicity occurs. The
counts are therefore the same
up to that sign. Varying the cap
length and the triangle data to the
ones defining $B(p,r)$ gives a
chain homotopy through the
one-dimensional moduli spaces.
The preglue-to-solution path is
within the pure section chart,
and determinant transport on that
path preserves the canonical
orientation of a regular solution.
This is the pure symplectic gluing
input of Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(c).
\end{proof}

This completes Theorem~\ref{thm:C}.
The statement uses the compression
cup of the manuscript. It does
not replace continuation by an
inverse of a chosen mixed map.

\subsection{Objects with vanishing homology}\label{subsec:zero}
Suppose $H(C_G(M))=0$. Lemma~\ref{lem:acyclic}
and Proposition~\ref{prop:N-isomorphism}
make both $C_G(M,p)$ and $\CF(M,p)$
contractible for every presentation.
Every closed map from or to a
contractible complex is a boundary:
compose it with a contracting
homotopy and use
\eqref{eq:compose-homotopy}. Hence
there is exactly one morphism
between it and any other object
in the homotopy category.

At such an object we take the
continuation and reflection maps
needed for the comparison to be
zero representatives of those
unique morphisms. In particular
a reflection map here is a
homotopy equivalence between zero
objects, not an invertible matrix.
We assert neither character
constancy nor existence of the
same configuration-level reflection
pairing at this object. Every
identity involving it is interpreted
in the homotopy category.

This convention also handles a
word passing through an acyclic
intermediate object: its class is
zero, as is the corresponding
gauge composite. Reduction modulo
two preserves a contracting
homotopy. Thus Paper~I's maps
incident to that object are
null-homotopic as well.

Nonzero mod-two gauge homology
implies nonzero integral gauge
homology, since an acyclic finite
free integral complex would be
contractible. Integral framed
calculations place rational homology
spheres and $\#^k(S^1\times S^2)$ in
the nonvanishing case: for a rational
homology sphere $Y$ the Euler
characteristic of $I^\#(Y)$ is
$|H_1(Y;\ZZ)|$
\cite[Corollary~1.4]{Scaduto}, and
$I^\#(\#^k(S^1\times S^2))$ is computed in
\cite[Section~7.8]{Scaduto}.
We do not use the converse
implication: a nonzero finite
odd-torsion integral group need
not be detected modulo two.

\section{Choices, primary naturality and handle maps}
\label{sec:choices}

\subsection{Order of the auxiliary choices}
All choices in a fixed comparison involve
finitely many presentations. The following
order avoids requiring a perturbation to
be simultaneously small relative to a
parameter chosen after it.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item Fix the splittings, metric data,
paired gauge bases and trace-coordinate
families for all objects and their
reflections. Choose a common primary
regularity threshold $r_H$ for this
finite set: the radius $r_*$ of
Proposition~\ref{prop:small-ball}.
\item Choose the first auxiliary heights
$t_*,t'_*$ so their height perturbations
and small generic corrections have norm
less than $r_H$. These data are used as
upper auxiliary presentations.
\item Choose the character-constancy
radius $r_\chi$ below $r_H$ and inside the
small-primary neighborhoods required
for those heights. Use the same choice
for a presentation and its reflection.
\item Choose smaller heights $t''$ for
the direct duality argument, $t_f$ for
the reflection fold, and $t_C$ for the
compression cup. Their height data
lie inside the corresponding balls.
Choose the lower datum of a cup after
$t_C$, sufficiently small relative to
that height.
\item Fix the ordinary gauge trajectory data and the symplectic
strip-end data for every required end, including the intermediate
closed cuts. Make the simultaneous regular end choices before
selecting their weights. Choose a common sufficiently small weight
below the relevant primal, adjoint and scalar thresholds. For
configuration reference paths it must also lie below their
remainder rates; relative end preparation then supplies faster
reference paths when needed. The completed-domain estimates at
these choices are Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(b).
\item Select the finite compact reference families needed for
coherence, and regularize the limiting vertex, triangle and
completed-core problems with these fixed ends. Their primary
transport, finite stabilizations and compact secondary supports
are fixed before taking a geometric limit. In particular an
auxiliary upper datum is a full presentation with its own
vertex-map thresholds. Keep the collapsing compression unperturbed,
including the dependence regions of the holonomy terms.
\item Choose the ordinary necks after these limiting data have
been fixed. Then choose the collapse scales from the bounds for
the compact reference families and the finite limiting index-zero
solution sets, with their decay rates and the already fixed
weights. For simultaneous determinant comparisons use the cofinal
neck-and-scale regime in Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family}. No uniform
threshold for all independent neck and collapse parameters is used.
\item At the resulting positive geometric parameters choose any
further compactly supported secondary regularizers within the
openness thresholds for the endpoint counts, preserving every
frozen end equation. Extend them relatively over the comparison
families. If an end datum or a limiting regularization is changed,
reselect the dependent necks and collapse scales and use the
corresponding data homotopies. Independence over the full permitted
weight range is applied only after this common small-weight
construction, as in Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(c).
\end{enumerate}
Every choice has a positive threshold
once the preceding finite data have
been fixed. Auxiliary upper data may
lie outside the final character ball;
the folded comparison supplies their
coherent continuation system directly.
The lower data and the handle target
where reflection is used lie inside
the relevant ball.

\subsection{Small-primary naturality}
We now prove the comparison for changes
of primary data. All maps in the next
two propositions are even.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:primary-naturality}
For regular $p,q$ in one admissible ball,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:primary-naturality}
 N_q\cont_G(p,q)\simeq
              \pm\cont_S(p,q)N_p.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Choose one upper height presentation $a$
of $-M$ generic for $\bar p$ and
$\bar q$. Apply the cup formula and
Proposition~\ref{prop:diagonal-cup} to
both fold pairs. They give
\begin{align*}
 \cont_S(\bar p,a)&\simeq
 \pm N_a\cont_G(\bar p,a)
                       n_{\bar p}\widehat\Phi_{\bar p},\\
 \cont_S(\bar q,a)&\simeq
 \pm N_a\cont_G(\bar q,a)
                       n_{\bar q}\widehat\Phi_{\bar q}.
\end{align*}
All factors being eliminated are
homotopy equivalences. Cancel the
common upper datum using continuation
composition. Thus
\begin{align*}
 \cont_S(\bar p,\bar q)\simeq\pm
 &(n_{\bar q}\widehat\Phi_{\bar q})^{-1}
       \cont_G(\bar p,\bar q)\\
 &\hspace{30mm}\cdot
       n_{\bar p}\widehat\Phi_{\bar p}.
\end{align*}
Use the reflection formula
\eqref{eq:cont-reflection} on the
symplectic side and its gauge analogue
from~\eqref{eq:gauge-duality}. The
symmetric conventions give
\[
 \widehat\Phi_{\bar p}=\Phi_p,
 \qquad \Phi_{G,M}n_{\bar p}=N_p^\vee,
\]
and the same equalities at $q$.
Cancelling these pairings transforms
the displayed identity into
\[
 \cont_S(q,p)^\vee\simeq
 \pm\bigl(N_p\cont_G(q,p)N_q^{-1}\bigr)^\vee.
\]
Transpose and rename $p,q$ to obtain
\eqref{eq:primary-naturality}.
Every double-dual map occurs together
with its inverse. Because the maps
here are even, the cancellations
introduce no grading-dependent sign.
\end{proof}

This proof uses the same pairing in
the diagonal-cup and reflection
identities. Choosing an unrelated
chain-level duality isomorphism would
leave a diagonal automorphism after
the cancellation.

\subsection{Mixed duality}
Put $N_p^\dagger=n_p\widehat\Phi_p$.
This is the transpose of the reflected
mixed map, with the gauge and
symplectic pairings inserted.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:mixed-duality}
For every regular $p$ in the admissible
ball,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mixed-duality}
 N_pN_p^\dagger\simeq\pm1.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
First take $p$ small enough for a fold
at the height $t''$, with upper datum
$r$ in the same ball. The two cup
identities give
\[
 \cont_S(p,r)\simeq
 \pm N_r\cont_G(p,r)n_p\widehat\Phi_p.
\]
By primary naturality the right side
is homotopic, up to sign, to
$\cont_S(p,r)N_pN_p^\dagger$.
Cancel the continuation equivalence.

For a general datum $q$ in the ball,
transpose primary naturality for the
reflected presentations. The symmetric
pairings give
\[
 N_q^\dagger\cont_S(p,q)\simeq
             \pm\cont_G(p,q)N_p^\dagger.
\]
Combine this with primary naturality
and~\eqref{eq:mixed-duality} at $p$:
\[
 N_qN_q^\dagger\cont_S(p,q)
             \simeq\pm\cont_S(p,q).
\]
Cancel the continuation once more.
The contractible case has the
interpretation of
Section~\ref{subsec:zero}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{One- and three-handle squares}
At the compatible handle data define
the integral compression cup by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:U}
 U_C=\check U_C\widehat\Phi_p:
               \CF(p)\longrightarrow\CF(q),
 \qquad q=C\circ p.
\end{equation}
Define the cap by reflected
transposition. If $\bar C$ is the
reflected compression and
$\check U_{\bar C}:\CF(p)^\vee\to\CF(\bar q)$
is its unpaired cup, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:V}
 \check V_C=\ev_{\CF(p)}^{-1}
                  (\check U_{\bar C})^\vee,
 \qquad V_C=\check V_C\widehat\Phi_q.
\end{equation}
Then $V_C:\CF(q)\to\CF(p)$. Equivalently
it is the transpose of the paired
reflected cup with the two pairings
inserted. These are the orientations
of the compression cup and cap counts
of Paper~I. At other presentations
they are conjugated by the geometric
continuation maps.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:handles}
For the one-handle cobordism $W_k$ and
its corresponding three-handle
cobordism $W_3$, one has
\begin{align}
 U_CN_p&\simeq\pm N_q\IG(W_k),
                                      \label{eq:onehandle}\\
 V_CN_q&\simeq\pm N_p\IG(W_3).
                                      \label{eq:threehandle}
\end{align}
The identities hold at all admissible
endpoint presentations in the balls of the
splittings of the handle data; other endpoints
are reached through Section~\ref{sec:descent}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The cup formula and mixed duality give
\[
 U_CN_p\simeq
 \pm N_q\IG(W_k)n_p\widehat\Phi_pN_p
 \simeq\pm N_q\IG(W_k),
\]
which proves~\eqref{eq:onehandle}.
For the cap, transpose the cup formula
at the reflected compression. The
graded evaluation maps and
$\Phi_{G,-M}=\Phi_{G,M}^\vee\ev$
give the gauge transpose of the
reversed handle map. Mixed duality
at $q$ cancels the reflected mixed
map. In detail, in the homotopy
category,
\begin{align*}
 V_CN_q
 &=\check V_C\widehat\Phi_qN_q\\
 &\simeq\pm\check V_C n_q^{-1}\\
 &\simeq\pm N_p\Phi_{G,-M}^{-1}
            \IG(\overline W_3)^\vee
            \Phi_{G,-M^+}\\
 &\simeq\pm N_p\IG(W_3).
\end{align*}
The only potentially odd map in the
transpose comparison is the handle
map; all pairings and mixed maps
are even. Its degree is retained
in the graded transpose, so any
remaining sign is scalar.

Finally conjugate both handle maps
by continuations from arbitrary
admissible endpoints to the
compatible handle data. Primary
naturality moves the mixed maps
past these continuations, and
gauge functoriality identifies the
gauge composite with the same
cobordism. Homotopies compose by
\eqref{eq:compose-homotopy}.
\end{proof}

\section{Descent to cobordisms and proof of the main theorem}
\label{sec:descent}

\subsection{The remaining changes of presentation}
The geometric presentation moves are the
ones in Paper~I. We record how the
integral orientations enter them.
A change of regularizing data uses
the family defining
Proposition~\ref{prop:N-isomorphism}.
A change of strip-end almost-complex
structure uses Proposition~\ref{prop:end-data}(ii),
with its actual outgoing continuation map and the
fixed transported end systems.
For a change of gauge base, both the
gauge and symplectic generator lines
are converted by the same
right-placed maps. The reference
constant pair has no extra line,
so its orientation comparison
commutes with this conversion.

An isomorphism of decorated data
pulls back the equation, its
linearization and the shifting
construction; the pullback of a
shift is a shift, and the
orientation maps do not depend on
the shifting path
(Proposition~\ref{prop:transport}). Choose pulled-back
stabilizations in the determinant
construction. The kernel gluing
maps then commute with the
isomorphism, and the mixed square
commutes exactly on chains at the
pulled-back data. A subsequent
continuation gives the square at
the originally chosen data, up to
sign and homotopy. This applies
to frame changes and diffeomorphisms
preserving the torus decoration.

A Heegaard stabilization is realized
by the cancelling one- and two-handle
pair of Paper~I. Let $r$ be the
stabilized presentation. The one-handle
square ends at the vertex $C\circ p$,
which carries height perturbations on
both bodies, while the two-handle square
holds from $(C\circ p)'$ to $r'$, the
presentations with the perturbed primary
data in the proof of Paper~I,
Proposition~12.4. As in Paper~I, insert the
continuations $\cont_S(C\circ p,(C\circ p)')$
and $\cont_S(r',r)$; their squares are
Proposition~\ref{prop:primary-naturality},
since $(C\circ p)'$ and $r'$ lie in the
relevant balls by the choices of
Section~\ref{sec:choices}. Compose
\eqref{eq:onehandle}, these two squares
and \eqref{eq:twohandle}. The decorated
gauge cobordism is the identity,
so its map is continuation up to
sign and homotopy. The reverse
stabilization is treated by the
cancelling two- and three-handle
pair. Passing through a common
stabilization treats changes of
metric data and isotopies of the
splitting. The order of choices
in Section~\ref{sec:choices} allows
the new vertices to satisfy the
integral height and ball conditions.
No orientation conclusion is
inferred from cancellation of
unsigned counts alone.

Thus every elementary presentation
arrow $e:p\to q$ has a symplectic
map $S_e$ constructed from the
curves of Paper~I and a square
\begin{equation}\label{eq:edge-square}
 N_qG_e\simeq\pm S_eN_p.
\end{equation}
The gauge arrows $G_e$ represent
the relevant decorated cobordism
or identity continuation. This
includes both even presentation
moves and possibly odd handle
arrows.

\subsection{Projective descent}
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:descent}
Suppose a connected presentation
category maps to a cobordism
category and every cobordism has
an elementary word. Let $G_e$
be a projective gauge functor on
these words. Suppose $N_p$ are
isomorphisms in a homotopy
category and the elementary
maps satisfy
\eqref{eq:edge-square}. Then
$S_d$ depends only on the
represented cobordism and the
endpoint presentations, up to
sign and homotopy.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For a word $d=e_m\cdots e_1$,
compose the squares to obtain
\[
 S_dN_{p_0}\simeq
     \pm N_{p_m}G_{e_m}\cdots G_{e_1}.
\]
Every composition of homotopies
is valid by
\eqref{eq:compose-homotopy}.
If $d'$ represents the same
cobordism, gauge functoriality
identifies the two gauge words
up to sign and homotopy. Cancel
$N_{p_0}$ to identify $S_d$
and $S_{d'}$.

For words representing the
identity cobordism this also
gives the transitive system of
comparison maps between
presentations. The identity
word is the identity map, and
concatenation of words gives
composition up to sign. Hence
these systems descend to a
projective functor and the
classes of $N_p$ give a
projective natural isomorphism.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:A}]
Proposition~\ref{prop:N-isomorphism}
gives the integral mixed
isomorphisms. Equation
\eqref{eq:twohandle},
Theorem~\ref{thm:handles},
Proposition~\ref{prop:primary-naturality}
for changes of primary data within a
ball, and the presentation comparisons
above supply all elementary
squares. Generation and
connectivity are the geometric
presentation results of
Paper~I, with the additional
smallness conditions arranged
as in Section~\ref{sec:choices}.
Proposition~\ref{prop:gauge-functor}
and Lemma~\ref{lem:descent}
therefore give
\eqref{eq:main} and the
projective functoriality.

The mixed maps preserve the
chosen object gradings. On
homology, the comparison with
the homogeneous gauge map
therefore gives the asserted
parity of the symplectic map.
No separate assertion of
homogeneity for every chosen
chain representative of a
long word is needed.

Modulo two all determinant
orientations give the same
counts. The conversions become
the identity on the generator
basis, the signs in the tensor
and dual formulas disappear,
and the pairing
\eqref{eq:Phi-S} becomes the
paired-basis reflection
pairing. Thus the geometric
maps reduce to those of
Paper~I at the same data. At
an acyclic object the zero
representatives reduce to
maps homotopic to those of
Paper~I by
Section~\ref{subsec:zero}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Scope and further questions}
Theorem~\ref{thm:A} assumes the unsigned analytic results of Paper~I.
The additional analysis used here, the linear foundations of
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations} and the relative preparation and
shifting of Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}, is proved in
Appendices~\ref{app:inputs} and~\ref{app:shifting}, and the oriented
torus comparison is derived in Section~\ref{sec:excision}. The ordinary
nonseparating self-gluing is supplied by
Proposition~\ref{prop:selfgluing}; its regularization uses only
collar-supported ordinary gauge perturbations.

Several further extensions ask
different questions. Exact
composition signs require more
than projectivizing the maps;
the regrading discussed after
\eqref{eq:gauge-dictionary}
indicates the extra grading
information involved. An
identification with integral
homology-oriented instanton
maps requires a comparison of
orientation systems on the
framed quotient. An
identification with integral
quilted Floer maps requires a
comparison with relative-spin
orientations on the compression
correspondences. None of these
identifications follows from
the integral theorem by
reducing modulo two.

\appendix
\section{Analytic foundations}\label{app:inputs}

This appendix proves Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}. All
references to Paper~I mean the companion version accompanying this
manuscript. The present paper does not supply an independent proof of
that manuscript's unsigned collapse, torus excision, or generation
results. It uses them as they are stated there, except where a proof
here says that it reruns an argument of Paper~I; the principal case is
Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family}, which lists the modified steps. The arguments below
combine the local theory of \cite{DFL,DFL-mixed} with explicit end
models. They are the local-plus-ends arguments of
\cite[Lemma~4.34]{DFL}, carried out on the domains of this paper.
Theorem, proposition and remark numbers in \cite{DFL-mixed} are those
of arXiv:2109.07038v1.

\subsection{Local theory and end models}\label{app:local}
\emph{Local estimates.} On a compact region meeting the matching seam,
with a product metric and the fixed almost-complex structure near the
seam, the local estimates for the mixed operator are
\cite[Theorem~4.17]{DFL}; they are uniform along $C^\infty$-convergent
sequences of configurations by \cite[Remark~4.19]{DFL}. For
domain-dependent almost-complex structures, and for the formal adjoint,
the local estimates and weak regularity are \cite[Theorem~5]{DFL-mixed},
with \cite[Remark~4.33]{DFL}; their uniformity on neighbourhoods of the
configuration and of the data, for both the operator and its adjoint,
is \cite[Remarks~5.73 and~5.76]{DFL-mixed}. Away from the seam these are
the interior and boundary estimates for the anti-self-duality operator
with the normal boundary condition and for real Cauchy--Riemann
operators with totally real boundary conditions. Holonomy perturbations
contribute zeroth-order terms with the bounds of
\cite[Proposition~6.1]{DFL}. The conformal factor of
Definition~\ref{def:realization} does not change the anti-self-duality
equation; it rescales the scalar row by $f^{-2}$ and adds zeroth-order
terms to it. A compact
family in the topology of Section~\ref{sec:geometry} has coefficients
converging in every $C^k$ norm on compact sets, so these estimates are
uniform on it.

\emph{Charts.} At a smooth configuration the matching-boundary chart is
built from \cite[Lemmas~4.1, 4.2 and~4.4]{DFL} and the construction
following Lemma~4.4, with the slice of \cite[Proposition~4.6]{DFL} and
the $L^q$ radius of Paper~I, Remark~5.2; the $L^2$ radius stated in
\cite[Lemma~4.1]{DFL} does not suffice. The construction is local, so
on a compact family the charts vary continuously with their centers.
Charts with a radius uniform as a torus length tends to infinity are
used only at the preglued references of Paper~I, Definition~10.23 and
Lemmas~10.27--10.28.

\emph{End models.} (O) At an ordinary gauge or strip end the operator is
translation invariant with invertible asymptotic operator, the extended
Hessian at a nondegenerate irreducible critical point or $J\partial_t$
with transverse boundary conditions. Weighted estimates hold for weights
below the gap, by \cite[Section~3.3.1]{Donaldson-book} for gauge ends
and by the same Fourier argument for strip ends. (E) At a mixed end the
stationary operator is self-adjoint with kernel the tangent space of the
primary critical family and a spectral gap
\cite[Section~4.2.1 and Proposition~4.21]{DFL}. On the torus-extended
slices $Y_{\mathsf R}$ and the joined slices $D_{i,\lambda}$ the gap is
uniform for lengths bounded below (Paper~I, Proposition~10.7), and on
compact ranges of lengths by continuity (proof of Paper~I,
Proposition~10.17). The moving boundary domain is transported to a fixed
one by \cite[Proposition~5.27 and Lemma~5.67]{DFL-mixed}, and the weighted
estimate for weights in $(0,\gamma)$ follows by Fourier transform in the
end coordinate, as in Step~1(E) of the proof of Paper~I,
Proposition~10.26. (T) On a flat torus region the operator and its
adjoint are the invertible operators of Paper~I, Proposition~10.3, with
the stated weighted bounds; at a flat face the normal derivatives are
recovered from the equation as in the proof of Paper~I, Theorem~10.5(b).

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(a)}
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:compact-remainder}
Let $\mathfrak F$ be a compact family as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}. There are a compact subset $K$ of the
cores and a constant $C$ such that, for every member and every $x$ in
the domain of its realized operator $D$ and every $\eta$ in the domain
of its weak adjoint,
\[
 \|x\|\le C\bigl(\|Dx\|+\|x\|_{L^2(K)}+|p(x)|\bigr),\qquad
 \|\eta\|\le C\bigl(\|D^*\eta\|+\|\eta\|_{L^2(K)}\bigr),
\]
where $p(x)$ denotes the stationary coordinates.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose cutoffs subordinate to a fixed compact core, the ordinary ends,
the mixed ends and, on torus domains, the torus region beyond a fixed
radius. Their gradients can be made small on the noncompact overlaps,
as in Step~2 of the proof of Paper~I, Proposition~10.26. On each end
apply the corresponding model of Section~\ref{app:local}; the
coefficients differ from the model by terms which are small beyond a
common truncation, uniformly on $\mathfrak F$. On the core apply the
local estimates. Summing and absorbing the small commutator and
coefficient terms gives the first estimate for the decaying part; the
finite stationary columns and the scalar row on them are bounded, which
accounts for $|p(x)|$. The adjoint estimate is the same argument with
the adjoint models, whose invertibility is recorded in
Section~\ref{app:local}.

Varying geometry is handled as follows. The collar identifications
$\psi_{\mathsf R}$ of Paper~I, Definition~10.1(a), are the identity outside
the region $N_X$, which is compact in the normal direction and extends
along both mixed ends; they change the mixed-end slices from $Y$ to
$Y_{\mathsf R}$. The width identifications in the proof of Paper~I,
Proposition~10.17, are the identity outside the bridge. Pulled back by
them, the metrics form a compact family of uniformly elliptic metrics,
and on the mixed ends model (E) is applied on the slices $Y_{\mathsf R}$
and $D_{i,\lambda}$ for lengths in the given compact range. At positive
collapse scale, the domains $X_\eps$, $\eps\in[\eps_1,\eps_2]$, of
Paper~I, Definition~6.2, are built in fixed coordinates, with section
complex structure $j_\eps$ and seam embedding $F_\eps$ depending smoothly
on $\eps$. An isotopy of the collar embeddings $F_\eps$, extended to a
diffeomorphism of the section surface, identifies them with $X_{\eps_1}$
as decorated manifolds. At each fixed $\eps>0$ the collapsing region is
compact with a smooth metric (proof of Paper~I, Proposition~6.9). The zero-scale limit domains are obtained, in
the proofs of Paper~I, Theorems~9.10 and~11.8, by joining DFL-type
domains of compression pairs, with the new mixed ends of Paper~I,
Proposition~6.9, and a polygon domain along ordinary strip ends; the
joined domain has a compact core, and the argument above applies to it
directly, with model (E) at the new ends. On the completed torus domains
a compact family is in torus normal form after the gauge transformations
of Lemma~\ref{lem:split-preglue}(i), and model (T) applies beyond a fixed
radius.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(a)]
Charts are recorded in Section~\ref{app:local}. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-remainder} and Rellich's theorem on $K$, each
realized operator has finite-dimensional kernel and closed range, and
so does its adjoint; hence it is Fredholm with cokernel the kernel of
the weak adjoint. Local regularity for the operator and its weak
adjoint makes these elements smooth. After transport to fixed spaces by
the boundary maps of Section~\ref{app:local} and the domain
identifications above, the coefficients vary continuously in operator
norm on the family, so the operators form continuous Fredholm families
and their determinant lines form continuous line bundles. For the
$W^{1,p}\to L^p$ realization, $p>2$, of the Cauchy--Riemann summand used
in unfolding in Section~\ref{sec:reflection}, the standard
$L^p$ theory of real Cauchy--Riemann operators with totally real
boundary conditions and nondegenerate ends gives the Fredholm property,
and elliptic regularity identifies its kernel and cokernel with those
of the Hilbert realization. The comparison of realizations is
Lemma~\ref{lem:realization}; its paths are compact families of this
kind, and its proof uses only the Fredholm and continuity statements
just proved. The affine-center comparison is
Proposition~\ref{prop:affine-square}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(b)}
The end critical sets are finite and nondegenerate, and the primary
critical families are compact and regular. Hence there is a common gap
for the finitely many end models of a compact family, and a common
small weight below all gaps and scalar thresholds; at mixed ends the
stationary coordinates are split off before the weight is imposed
\cite[Section~4.2.1]{DFL}.

\emph{The scalar operator.} The Neumann operator $d_A^*d_A$ is Fredholm by
the argument of Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-remainder}: its end models have
positive gaps by irreducibility of the end connections, and on the
torus region the fibre operator has mass $\mu_T$ because
$H^0(T;\ad\alpha)=0$. Integration by parts identifies its kernel at
weight zero with parallel sections, which vanish on an irreducible
collar; it is self-adjoint of index zero there, and sufficiently small
weights preserve the index and add no kernel. This is the argument in
the proof of Paper~I, Theorem~5.6(ii). Near the preglued references the
uniform version is the proof of Paper~I, Lemma~10.25, which is stated for
completed index-zero solutions and applies without change to the
constant pairs and to the references along the shifting rectangles.

A regular zero-dimensional solution has an invertible realized operator
by definition, and this does not depend on the realization by
Lemma~\ref{lem:realization}. Uniformity for the preglued families as the
torus lengths tend to infinity is Lemma~\ref{lem:bridge-gluing}. At
positive collapse scale the estimates are those of
Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-remainder} on compact scale intervals; nothing
uniform as the scale tends to zero is claimed beyond
Lemma~\ref{lem:collapse-family}. The constant reference pairs are
treated in the next lemma. For small secondary or inhomogeneous data the
operators remain invertible by openness, with the bounds of
Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-remainder}.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:constant-pairs}
The constant reference pairs, with no secondary or inhomogeneous data,
have invertible physical operators on every DFL-type domain of a
transverse compression pair in the class of Paper~I, Lemma~9.7
(including the vertex domain and the truncated output regions of the
triangles), on the separate domains $W_{a,\mathsf R}$,
$\mathsf R\in[0,\infty]$, on the joined domains $W^J_\lambda$, $\lambda>0$,
and on the closed-cut piece $M_\lambda$, including along the family used
in the proof of Paper~I, Lemma~9.7, as applied in Paper~I,
Proposition~10.21. The inverse bounds are uniform on compact families of
lengths, and the positive elements extend continuously through torus
completion.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\emph{The quadratic identity.} Write $d^h_A$ for the perturbed exterior
derivative of \cite[(4.42)]{DFL}, which adds the primary Hessian term on
the compression cylinders and equals $d_A$ on the flat genus and torus
regions. For every physical variation $(\zeta,\nu)$ satisfying the
linearized matching and arc conditions, with the stationary behavior of
the realization,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:quadratic-identity}
 \int_X\tr(d^h_A\zeta\wedge d^h_A\zeta)+\int_U\Omega(d\nu,d\nu)=0 .
\end{equation}
On the original pieces this is \cite[Lemma~4.40]{DFL}: the interior terms
are total derivatives by the symmetry of the primary Hessian and the
primary equation, the matching terms cancel because the surface trace is
closed with class the section value, and the arc terms vanish on the
Lagrangian tangent spaces. A primary tangent vector satisfies the
linearized primary equation, so its pure stationary contribution at a
truncated mixed end vanishes; the terms involving the decaying remainder
tend to zero with the truncation, and the ordinary ends contribute zero.
The flat torus region contributes only Stokes terms. At a finite outer
torus face the conditions are $\zeta(\partial_r)=0$ and
$d_\alpha(\zeta|_T)=0$ (Paper~I, Definition~10.23). Write the trace as
$a+\psi\,d\theta$ with $d_\alpha a=0$; since $H^1(T;\ad\alpha)=0$ we have
$a=d_\alpha f$, and
$\int_T\tr(a\wedge\partial_\theta a)=\int_T\tr(d_\alpha f\wedge
d_\alpha\partial_\theta f)=0$ by Stokes on $T$. The cross term
$\int_T\tr(a\wedge d_T\psi)$ vanishes by Stokes on $T$ because
$d_\alpha a=0$, as in \cite[(4.52)]{DFL}. On a completed domain use spatial
cutoffs equal to one on all primary supports, whose gradients are of
size $C/R$ on the flat tail; the resulting errors tend to zero because
the tail field is in $H^1$. On a joined domain the bridge has no
boundary, and its interfaces cancel those of the two cores. On the
closed-cut piece the ordinary input $Z$ contributes nothing.

\emph{The kernel.} Let $x$ be in the kernel and let $(\zeta,\nu)$ be its
physical variation. The mixed equations give $(d^h_A\zeta)^+=0$ and the
linear Cauchy--Riemann equation for $\nu$, so both integrands in
\eqref{eq:quadratic-identity} are nonnegative with the trace convention
of \cite{DFL}. Hence $d^h_A\zeta=0$ and $d\nu=0$. The section variation
decays at its strip output, so $\nu=0$ on each component. The section
limit of the stationary coordinates is their image in the tangent space
of the primary Lagrangian, and the restriction map is an embedding, so
all stationary coordinates vanish. What remains is a decaying $\zeta$
with $d^h_A\zeta=0$, $d_A^*\zeta=0$ and the normal and face conditions.

\emph{A primitive.} Construct $\eta$ with $d_A\eta=\zeta$ as in
\cite[Proposition~4.54]{DFL}. On each compression cylinder take the
temporal primitive from the gauge input; it converges because the
kernel field solves the full gauge-fixed equation on the input
cylinder, whose asymptotic operator is nondegenerate, so the field
decays exponentially there. The primary equation and Hessian symmetry
make it a primitive of the spatial part. On a flat genus product the
surface class of $\zeta$ is $\nu=0$, and the surface scalar inverse
gives the primitive; the base components agree because a parallel
adjoint scalar on an odd surface vanishes. On the flat torus region put
$\eta_T=\Delta_\alpha^{-1}d_\alpha^*\zeta_T$ fibrewise. Since
$H^1(T;\ad\alpha)=0$ this is a primitive of the fibre part, and since
$H^0(T;\ad\alpha)=0$ the base components agree. The primitives agree on
overlaps for the same reason, also across a joined bridge. The fibre
estimate $\|\eta_T\|\le\mu_T^{-1}\|\zeta_T\|$, the scalar spatial
estimates on the mixed ends and the irreducible gauge input put $\eta$
in the weighted $H^1$ space. Integration by parts with exhausting cutoffs
gives
$\|\zeta\|^2=\langle d_A\eta,\zeta\rangle=\langle\eta,d_A^*\zeta\rangle=0$;
the boundary term vanishes by the normal and face conditions. Thus the
kernel is zero.

\emph{The index.} On the special quintuple \cite[Proposition~3.19]{DFL}
gives index zero for the realization of \cite{DFL}. That realization and
the one of Definition~\ref{def:realization} use the same decaying space and
the same stationary parameter space; their stationary columns, including
the scalar ones, differ by a bounded finite-rank map, so the indices
agree (as in Paper~I, the remark after Definition~10.23); a change of
weight inside the gap does not change the index (Lemma~\ref{lem:realization}).
If the almost-complex structure near the matching line is not the one of
\cite{DFL}, a homotopy of compatible structures localized in the seam
collar keeps the constant pair a solution and the operators Fredholm, by
Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-remainder}. For a DFL-type domain in the class of
Paper~I, Lemma~9.7, and for the closed-cut piece along the family in the
proof of that lemma, the constant pair remains a solution: on each mixed
end the metric is conformal to a product, and the perturbed
anti-self-duality condition on two-forms is conformally invariant; the
flat references do not depend on the end metric and the primary critical
set does not depend on the metric or the almost-complex structure (proof
of Paper~I, Lemma~9.7); and the section is constant. The index is
therefore constant along the family. For $W_{a,\mathsf R}$ with finite $\mathsf R$ the index is
constant along the collar-length family (Paper~I, Theorem~10.13(i)). By
Lemma~\ref{lem:split-preglue}(iii), which does not use the present
lemma, $P_{\mathsf R}(C_{a,x})$ is the constant pair on
$W_{a,\mathsf R}$ and $P_\lambda(C_{-,x_-},C_{+,x_+})=C^{(\lambda)}_{J,x}$.
Hence Lemma~\ref{lem:bridge-gluing}(ii) gives $\ind D_{\mathsf R}=\ind D_\infty=0$
for large $\mathsf R$, and the index at $C^{(\lambda)}_{J,x}$ is the value
$0$ at $\lambda=\infty$ for large $\lambda$; it is constant in
$\lambda\in(0,\infty)$ by Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(a). In all
cases the kernel is zero and the index is zero, so the operator is
invertible.

\emph{Uniformity.} On a compact family of finite lengths the inverses
vary continuously. Through torus completion the positive elements are
continuous by Lemma~\ref{lem:bridge-gluing}(iii), and uniform inverse
bounds for large lengths follow from its part (i), with $E=0$ since the
limiting operators are onto.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:foundations}(c)}
The weight comparison is Proposition~\ref{prop:weights}.

\emph{Compactness and gluing of pure moduli spaces.} The compression-body
Lagrangians are simply connected with $\pi_2=0$, and $\pi_2(\Msf_h)\cong\ZZ$
is generated by a class of Maslov index four; in the product target the
correspondence $L_C$ is simply connected (Paper~I, Section~11.3) and has
$\pi_2$ generated by spheres of zero area and zero Chern number
(Section~\ref{sec:characters}). The pairs and correspondences are
therefore monotone with minimal Maslov number at least three: for the
compression-body Lagrangians this is \cite[Proposition~2.27]{DFL}, and for
the correspondence $L_C$ it is Paper~I, Section~11.3. Sphere and disc bubbling has
codimension at least two in the dimensions used. The compactification
and boundary structure of the one-dimensional pure continuation, cap and
strip moduli spaces are then as in \cite[Theorem~3.9(c)]{WW-relative}.
Transversality there is obtained from Hamiltonian perturbations; in the
perturbation class used here, with zero Hamiltonian and
domain-dependent almost-complex structures, it is Paper~I,
Lemma~11.3, for the strips and triangles from which the cup and cap
maps are built, and Proposition~\ref{prop:end-data}(ii) for
continuation strips; the ordinary Heegaard strips are regular by the
definition of the symplectic complex (Paper~I, Section~3.4). Strip gluing
is \cite[Theorem~5.4.1]{Simcevic},
with \cite[Theorems~3.3 and~3.7]{MWW} and \cite{Mau} for the quilted
disc families, and exponential decay on half-strips is
\cite[Corollary~2.3.2]{Simcevic}. For a finite collection of regular
configurations with nondegenerate strip ends, every nearby
zero-dimensional solution at a long neck is the unique correction of a
preglued configuration, the correction tends to zero with the neck
length, and the estimates are uniform over compact families. Along the
path from the preglue to the solution the operators are invertible, by
the uniform right inverse at the preglue and the smallness of the
correction, as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:gluing-path}; hence
determinant transport preserves the canonical orientation. The unsigned
low-dimensional counts agree with those of Paper~I, Section~11.

\emph{Interior nodes.} Linear gluing of Cauchy--Riemann determinant lines
along strip ends is Lemma~\ref{lem:line-gluing}. For a family of real
Cauchy--Riemann operators on surfaces degenerating at an interior node,
the determinant lines form a topological line bundle
\cite[Proposition~2.4.12]{WW-orientations}, and the line isomorphism is
given by the exact sequence (18) of \cite[Theorem~2.4.5]{WW-orientations}
and \cite[Corollary~2.4.7]{WW-orientations}; it is associative
\cite[Proposition~2.4.8]{WW-orientations}. The sequence is proved there
for a boundary node, and the interior case is treated in the same way.
Its middle term is the fibre of the bundle at the node, a complex vector
space; it is even and canonically oriented, so the node contributes no
additional sign. Numbering is as in arXiv:1503.07803v6. These statements
localize, by the following stabilization argument. Choose stabilizations of a
mixed or pure operator supported away from a disc containing the node and
separated from the matching and boundary collars. Then the stabilized
kernel near the node is obtained by the local gluing map of the cited
proposition, and the rest of the operator is unchanged. Hence the line
bundle structure and the gluing isomorphism hold for the real
Cauchy--Riemann part of any such operator. Associativity follows from the cited
associativity, with disjoint supports.
Commutation with ordinary strip gluing follows because the two
stabilizations and cutoffs have disjoint supports, as in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:bridge-gluing}(iv). A cohomological composition identity
alone is not used as a substitute for these moduli-space gluing
statements.

\subsection{Boundary Hodge theory and holonomy regularity}
For a flat orthogonal bundle on
a compact compression body,
the input needed in
Proposition~\ref{prop:small-ball}
is the Hodge decomposition on
$W^{1,2}$ with normal boundary
condition. Its harmonic fields
are smooth and finite
dimensional, and its exact
part is the covariant
differential of $W^{2,2}$
sections. Since
$H^0(C;\ad)=0$, the Neumann
Green operator $G_N$ for
$d_B^*d_B$ exists and
preserves smoothness, so
$1-d_BG_Nd_B^*$ is a
smoothness-preserving
projection onto coclosed forms
satisfying the normal boundary
condition.
These statements follow from
the flat covariant de Rham
case of
\cite[Section~1.6 and
Theorems~3.10--3.11]{KL}; the
normal and tangential versions
are interchanged by Hodge star.

The boundary elliptic estimate
has the form
\[
 \|b\|_{W^{1,p}}\le C
 (\|d_Bb\|_{L^p}+\|d_B^*b\|_{L^p}
                              +\|b\|_{L^p})
\]
for $*b|_{\partial C}=0$.
For smooth fixed $B$, adjoint
bundle coefficients add only
bounded zeroth-order terms in
local trivializations. The
compactness and Coulomb-slice
inputs are Theorems A, D(a)
and F of the introductory
statements in
\cite{Wehrheim-book}.

Finite holonomy functions have
bounded gradients and
Lipschitz gradient differences
as used in
\eqref{eq:primary-norm}
\cite[Proposition~6.1]{DFL}.
Their higher derivatives obey
the local tame estimates
obtained by differentiating
parallel transport. Combined
with the boundary elliptic
estimate, these give the
bootstrap from the slice
fixed point to the smooth
perturbed-flat solution.
For holonomy families with
moving basepoints, restriction
and composition are performed
in the smooth topology, not
asserted bounded on arbitrary
low-order Sobolev spaces.

\subsection{Shifting and self-gluing}
Relative preparation and shifting are proved in
Appendix~\ref{app:shifting}. Naturality under isomorphisms of the
decorated data is automatic: the pullback of a shift is a shift, and the
orientation maps are independent of the shifting path
(Proposition~\ref{prop:transport}).

For ordinary gauge self-gluing,
Proposition~\ref{prop:selfgluing} applies the low-dimensional
instanton compactification, verifies matching on the prescribed
bundle and identifies the lifting fiber. Its orientation comparison
first traces over the full determinant-one torus complex and then
takes the larger quotient. Together with the finite group calculation
in Section~\ref{sec:excision}, this gives integral unit multiplicity.

\section{Relative preparation and shifting}\label{app:shifting}

This appendix proves Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting}. The one-input
statement is the family version of \cite[Lemmas~4.62 and~4.64]{DFL},
whose proof is sketched there; we give it in the topology of
Section~\ref{sec:geometry}, jointly continuous in configurations and
data, relative to the terminal subspace, and on the domains of this
paper. Throughout, a family is a continuous map from a compact metrizable
space $K$ to actual configurations on a fixed bundle, together with
continuously varying data. No quotient by gauge transformations is
taken; the lifting groups enter only through
Proposition~\ref{prop:transport}.

\emph{The terminal subspace.} For an output label $x$, a
\emph{representative} of the critical point over $x$ is a connection
$c=B\cup\mathrm{pr}^*b\cup B'$ on $Y_\#$, where $b$ is a flat connection
on $\Sigma$ over the point of $\mathcal M(\Sigma)$ determined by $x$ and
$B,B'$ are primary solutions on $Y_0,Y_0'$ which are products with value
$b$ on the collars $[-5/2,-2]\times\Sigma$ of their boundaries fixed in
Section~\ref{app:shift-prep}. Every such $c$ is a critical
point of the perturbed Chern--Simons functional in the gauge orbit of
$a_x$ or of its other lift, and the constant pair $C^c$ built from it is
equivalent to $C_x$ under the lifting group, by a gauge transformation
when $c$ lies in the orbit of $a_x$. Its operator is therefore invertible, its
positive element corresponds to that of $C_x$ under conjugation, and the
almost-complex gauge orientations are invariant under the bundle
automorphism. The lines at $c$ are identified with those at $a_x$ by this
automorphism. The identification agrees with transport along any path of
connections from $a_x$ to $c$: the almost-complex orientation of the
gauge line is invariant under the full group of bundle automorphisms,
of every degree and lifting class (Section~\ref{sec:gauge},
\cite[Section~5.1]{KM-unknot}), and the character of the conversion line
depends only on the lifting class
(Proposition~\ref{prop:character-gauge}), so it is trivial for
automorphisms of trivial lifting class, of any degree. For a
representative in the orbit of the other lift, the relating automorphism
has lifting class in the framing group, on which the character is
trivial by the last clause of Proposition~\ref{prop:character-gauge},
since all structures agree with the fixed germ near the odd torus; at
the cut $Z$ this is~\eqref{eq:cut-Chern}. The terminal subspace $\mathcal T_x$ consists of the
configurations which, outside the gauge input end, agree with $C^c$ for
some representative $c$; they are gauge paths ending at $c$ followed by
$C^c$. Proposition~\ref{prop:shifting} is to be read with this terminal
subspace. It cannot be replaced by the configurations ending at one fixed
$C_x$: those have a single topological energy, whereas attaching a sphere
of nonzero area changes the topological energy of a configuration without
changing its labels $(a,x)$.

\subsection{Reduction to a special quintuple}\label{app:shift-reduction}
Let $(Y,Y')$ be a pair of odd compression bodies with common boundary
$\Sigma$, a surface with two components on each of which the bundle is
odd (Paper~I, Definition~3.2), with primary data whose Lagrangians
$L(Y),L(Y')\subset\mathcal M(\Sigma)$ are embedded (Paper~I, Section~3.2).
For the torus domains $\Sigma=\Sigma_h\sqcup T$; for the closed-cut piece
and the product-target vertex pairs $\Sigma=\Sigma_h\sqcup\Sigma_g$. Let
$X_D$, $U_+$ be the gauge region and section domain of the special
quintuple \cite[Section~3.1]{DFL}: the gauge region is
$\RR\times Y_0\cup U_-\times\Sigma\cup\RR\times(-Y_0')$, the matching seam is
$U_\partial\times\Sigma$, the Lagrangian arcs $\eta_+,\eta'_+$ carry $L(Y)$
and $L(Y')$, and the gauge input end is $(-\infty,-3]\times Y_\#$. Every
flat connection on $\Sigma$ is irreducible with central stabilizer.

The configuration space of Section~\ref{sec:geometry} depends only on the
decorated smooth manifold (bundle, matching seam, arcs with their
Lagrangians, ends with their limits) and on the primary data. It does
not depend on the metric, the almost-complex structure or the secondary
